## AI Research

### [China Derangement Syndrome](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/m4vLvzEMAqw5swPYN/china-derangement-syndrome-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Often I see people claim it’s essential for America to win the AI race against China (in whatever sense) for reasons like these:</span></p><ul><li><span>“What is the reason we want America to win the AI race? It’s </span><a href="https://x.com/AlecStapp/status/2031842273567392036"><span>because we want to make sure free open societies can defend themselves”</span></a><span> (Alec Stapp)</span></li><li><span>“We should seek to win the race to global AI technological superiority and ensure that China does not… </span><a href="https://x.com/pmarca/status/1666113539885658112"><span>to ensure that our way of life is not displaced by the much darker Chinese vision</span></a><span>“ (Marc Andreessen)</span></li><li><span>“Will it be one in which the United States and allied nations advance a global AI that spreads the technology’s benefits and opens access to it, or an authoritarian one, in which </span><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2024/07/25/sam-altman-ai-democracy-authoritarianism-future/"><span>nations or movements that don’t share our values use AI to cement and expand their power</span></a><span>?” (Sam Altman)</span></li><li><span>“In </span><i><span>Machines of Loving Grace</span></i><span>, I discussed the possibility that </span><a href="https://www.darioamodei.com/essay/the-adolescence-of-technology"><span>authoritarian governments might use powerful AI to surveil or repress their citizens in ways that would be extremely difficult to reform or overthrow</span></a><span>. Current autocracies are limited in how repressive they can be by the need to have humans carry out their orders, and humans often have limits in how inhumane they are willing to be. But AI-enabled autocracies would not have such limits.” (Dario Amodei)</span></li><li><span>“</span><a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/parting-thoughts/"><span>The torch of liberty will not survive</span></a><span> Xi getting AGI first. (And, realistically, American leadership is the only path to safe AGI, too.)” (Leopold Ashenbrenner)</span></li><li><span>“whoever wins the race for AI, that nation’s values are going to be reflected in AI. If China wins the race for AI, </span><a href="https://www.commerce.senate.gov/index.php/2025/9/we-must-do-both-commerce-chairman-ted-cruz-talks-ai-acceleration-risk-management-at-axios-and-politico-summits"><span>AI will be a tool for global surveillance and control</span></a><span> as carried out by a communist nation” (Ted Cruz)</span></li></ul><p><span>Those claims slide between a few different actual threat models:</span></p><ol><li><b><span>Government Capture by China: </span></b><span>China will overthrow and control the US government, maybe as part of general domination of the whole world.</span></li><li><b><span>Defeat in Cold War: </span></b><span>China will have greater wealth and prestige, so just as our prestige inspires many parts of the world to adopt our way of life today, much of the world will adopt the Chinese governance and cultural models instead.</span></li><li><b><span>Protection From Our Conquest: </span></b><span>China will fortify its own regime, so that it can’t be overthrown, whereas if we win the AI race, we can promptly overthrow the Chinese government and replace it with a new regime aligned with our values.</span></li></ol><p><span>The Dario quote points to (3) with unusual directness. The </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/race"><span>“race rather than slowdown” ending</span></a><span> of AGI 2027 also supposes that our AI lead will create interest in overthrowing the Chinese government. But most of the quotes I gave as examples above are interpreted as (1): that an AI-enabled Chinese government would overthrow Western governments.</span></p><p><span>My main point here is that (1) seems unfounded to me. </span><b><span>China is not an aggressive nation at all.</span></b><span> As far as I can tell, China has literally never attacked a non-bordering country in its entire history, nor have they ever tried to overthrow a foreign government by covert or manipulative means. China is also unique among nuclear powers for its unconditional no-first-use policy, which at face value implies they would withhold a nuclear response to even an overwhelming conventional invasion. Further:</span></p><ul><li><span>The Chinese haven’t built a network of military bases abroad or binding military alliances; they have a single foreign base in Djibouti and a single mutual-defense treaty with North Korea. In contrast, America maintains over 700 bases and a huge alliance network with NATO and the Asian military allies Japan, South Korea, Australia, and the Philippines.</span></li><li><span>Chinese military spending is 1.7% of GDP, versus 2.1% for France and 3.4% for America. Chinese foreign-aid spending is 0.07% of GDP versus the much larger 0.8% for France and 1.2% for America.</span></li><li><span>China has almost no history of covertly backing palace coups abroad, in contrast to America, Russia, and France.</span></li></ul><p><span>More broadly, China is a very inward-looking country compared to other major powers. Only 0.1% of Chinese residents were born abroad, much fewer than the 15% in America and 14% in France, fewer even than the 0.3% and 3% in India and Japan respectively. The Chinese government has peacefully compromised on almost all border disputes in central and southeast Asia, often taking a minority of the contested territory. (The Indian border is the exception.)</span></p><p><span>To many American voters and elites, tracing back to Woodrow Wilson more than 100 years ago, “the justification of America’s international role was messianic: America had an obligation, not to the balance of power, but to spread its principles throughout the world” (</span><a href="https://www.howardnema.com/wp-content/uploads/2016/08/Kissinger-Diplomacy-1.pdf"><span>Kissinger</span></a><span>). That isn’t the historical attitude of the Chinese government, whose leaders perceive foreign intervention or expansion as threatening to Chinese identity and culture.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>American exceptionalism is missionary. It holds that the United States has an obligation to spread its values to every part of the world. China’s exceptionalism is cultural. China does not proselytize; it does not claim that its contemporary institutions are relevant outside China.</span><br /><span>— Kissinger’s </span><a href="https://en.wikiquote.org/wiki/Henry_Kissinger#2010s"><i><span>On China</span></i></a></p></blockquote><p><span>It’s true that China doesn’t practice liberal governance. The core of liberalism is freedom of contract, limitations on government interference, and equal access to independent courts. In China, the CCP explicitly rejects limited government and exercises highly invasive control over business, speech, association, and religion. In China there’s no private ownership of land and no independent judiciary.</span></p><p><span>If you think it’s prudent to disable and overthrow the Chinese government when it becomes achievable militarily, then that’s certainly one (bellicose) position you could hold. Then you could say that a downside of losing the AI race is that the CCP may defend itself. But it’s unwise to project this ideological aggression onto the CCP itself without evidence.</span></p><p><b><span>Addendum: </span></b><span>It would have been mistaken for a European to say, in 1895, "Who cares about American industrialization? They have almost no army and have barely left their far-away continent." Soon afterward that European might find the Americans replacing his regime or dismantling his empire. So a counterargument here is that in general, countries that become wealthy and militarily powerful become aggressive regardless of how passive they seemed before. Under this reasoning, China has had limited imperial ambitions in the past only because it e.g. lacked naval superiority. This has to be an argument based on a general view of human nature and government.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/m4vLvzEMAqw5swPYN/china-derangement-syndrome-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Finding features in Transformers: Contrastive directions elicit stronger low-level perturbation responses than baselines](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QrPxNDprJwXiE73Py/finding-features-in-transformers-contrastive-directions)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Note: This is a research update sharing preliminary results as part of ongoing work.</span></i></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3249af30163624d9956e90a33b1d61ebe4e2570626398b907cb1705ba109c92a/z1hbxbetvykzxhzx2gaj" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 1: Contrastive (difference-of-means, English→Mandarin) feature directions elicit a downstream response at much smaller perturbation magnitudes than SAE directions, which behave similarly to random directions. This holds across multiple models and experimental setups.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Summary &amp; Main Results</span></h1><p><span>Understanding how concepts are represented in LLM internals would be extremely useful for AI safety (generally understanding AI's thinking, AI control and monitoring, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w4aeAFzSAguvqA5qu/how-to-go-from-interpretability-to-alignment-just-retarget" rel="noreferrer"><span>retargeting the search</span></a><span>). We assume that concepts are represented as directions in activation space (“features”). The most popular feature-finding approach, sparse dictionary learning via sparse autoencoders, suffers from strong dataset dependence (e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.17727v1"><span>Heap et al. 2025</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rtp6n7Z23uJpEH7od/saes-are-highly-dataset-dependent-a-case-study-on-the"><span>Kissane et al. 2024</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZBjhp6zwfE8o8yfni/stefanhex-s-shortform?commentId=DGWrmJEyZp7jFop5s"><span>Heimersheim 2024</span></a><span>). It is thus unclear whether the directions they find reflect the model's computations or properties of the training distribution.</span></p><p><span>Instead, we propose identifying feature directions by perturbing activations along a direction and using the model's response to judge whether the direction corresponds to a feature (following </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ioPnHKFyy4Cw2Gr2x/mechanistically-eliciting-latent-behaviors-in-language-1"><span>Mack &amp; Turner 2024</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LajDyGyiyX8DNNsuF/interim-research-report-activation-plateaus-and-sensitive-1"><span>Heimersheim &amp; Mendel, 2024</span></a><span>). We hypothesize that perturbations into random directions are suppressed, as they only produce interference noise along feature directions, while perturbations into feature directions create a focused perturbation and elicit a strong model response. If this is the case, we should see a longer suppression region for random directions than for feature directions.</span></p><p><b><span>Our novel contribution is that we test this hypothesis using contrastive probing/steering vectors.</span></b><span> We construct contrastive vectors (difference-of-means) for language (English→Mandarin), programming language (Python→Haskell), and gender (male→female) across different model families (Gemma 2-9B, Llama 3.1-8B, and Qwen 3-1.7B). We then perturb the models into contrastive-, SAE feature- and random directions</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#ftnt1"><span>[1]</span></a><span>, and compare the model responses shown in Figure 1. We find that contrastive directions indeed elicit a much stronger response, while SAE features and random directions require much larger magnitudes for a similar response strength. </span><b><span>To our knowledge, this is the first example of candidate features eliciting qualitatively different model responses.</span></b><span> (Though </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ioPnHKFyy4Cw2Gr2x/mechanistically-eliciting-latent-behaviors-in-language-1"><span>Mack &amp; Turner 2024</span></a><span> previously showed something close to the inverse: that directions which elicit different model responses often behave like steering vectors.)</span></p><p><span>Figure 2 shows perturbation curves for two additional contrastive vectors (male→female, Python→Haskell) and two additional models (Llama 3.1-8B, Qwen 3-1.7B) which produce equivalent results. In all cases we perturb activations at an early residual stream layer</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#ftnt2"><span>[2]</span></a><span>. We keep the activation norm constant and perturb at constant angular speed (following </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LajDyGyiyX8DNNsuF/interim-research-report-activation-plateaus-and-sensitive-1"><span>Heimersheim &amp; Mendel, 2024</span></a><span>). We measure the model response as the L2 distance between perturbed and unperturbed activations at the second-to-last layer. We show all individual response curves as thin lines, and the median as thick lines.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="figure2_composite.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1774020926/lexical_client_uploads/kfdypvt14jecechdtwjb.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 2: Perturbation response across multiple models and different contrastive pairs. Top left: Gemma 2-9B, male→female. Top right: Gemma 2-9B, Python→Haskell. Bottom left: Llama 3.1-8B, English→Mandarin. Bottom right: Qwen 3-1.7B, English→Mandarin.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Across all combinations, we observe that perturbing along the difference-of-means direction breaks through the suppression region at much smaller magnitudes than perturbing along random directions or SAE features. Perturbations into SAE feature directions look similar to random perturbations in most cases.</span></p><p><b><span>Interpretation:</span></b><span> We see point 1. as our main contribution; points 2. and 3. are consistent with past activation plateau literature.</span></p><ol><li><span>The fact that contrastive vectors, which are our best guess for some true feature directions, show a different response curve makes us hopeful that this activation perturbation-based agenda has a chance at identifying model features!</span></li><li><span>Our SAE results (confirming </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dS5dSgwaDQRoWdTuu/investigating-sensitive-directions-in-gpt-2-an-improved"><span>Lee &amp; Heimersheim 2024</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cF7TFw9FyDFWjk2FW/evaluating-synthetic-activations-composed-of-sae-latents-in"><span>Giglemiani et al. 2024</span></a><span>) show that SAE directions aren’t treated specially by the model. This is evidence against SAEs finding the true computational features of a model.</span></li><li><span>The observation of an initial “activation plateau” (confirming </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LajDyGyiyX8DNNsuF/interim-research-report-activation-plateaus-and-sensitive-1"><span>Heimersheim &amp; Mendel, 2024</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sGmT6aJPY7Xzuz3Ax/characterizing-stable-regions-in-the-residual-stream-of-llms"><span>Janiak et al. 2024</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WMfSbt7AAcJdHzysB/activation-plateaus-where-and-how-they-emerge"><span>Shinkle &amp; Heimersheim 2025</span></a><span>) might be evidence of error correction as proposed by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05451"><span>Hänni et al., 2024</span></a><span> (though see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/lawrencec?mention=user"><span>@LawrenceC</span></a><span>'s comment </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LajDyGyiyX8DNNsuF/interim-research-report-activation-plateaus-and-sensitive-1?commentId=ngdwsJCpTncG2NLDz"><span>here</span></a><span>).</span></li></ol><h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>Understanding how LLMs represent concepts internally would be extremely useful for AI safety as it would allow for a deeper understanding of model behaviour and better alignment research </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/epjuxGnSPof3GnMSL/alignment-remains-a-hard-unsolved-problem" rel="noreferrer"><span>feedback loops</span></a><span>, monitoring model cognition for undesired processes (e.g. sophisticated lie detection, AI control), and potential future alignment ideas (e.g. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w4aeAFzSAguvqA5qu/how-to-go-from-interpretability-to-alignment-just-retarget"><span>retarget the search</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>We assume that concepts are represented as directions in activation space (</span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2024/july-update/index.html"><span>Olah, 2024</span></a><span>). Under superposition (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.10652"><span>Elhage et al. 2022</span></a><span>), a model represents many more features than it has dimensions. This means that features cannot be fully orthogonal, and hence a perturbation along a feature direction will also have components along other feature directions (“interference”). </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05451"><span>Hänni et al., 2024</span></a><span> claim that, to perform meaningful computation in superposition, interference must be suppressed, i.e., the model must implement some form of error correction.</span></p><p><span>The most popular feature-finding approach, sparse dictionary learning via sparse autoencoders, suffers from strong dataset dependence (e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.17727v1"><span>Heap et al. 2025</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rtp6n7Z23uJpEH7od/saes-are-highly-dataset-dependent-a-case-study-on-the"><span>Kissane et al. 2024</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZBjhp6zwfE8o8yfni/stefanhex-s-shortform?commentId=DGWrmJEyZp7jFop5s"><span>Heimersheim 2024</span></a><span>). It is unclear whether the directions it finds reflect the model's computations, or properties of the training distribution. This motivates finding a data-independent test for whether directions are computationally meaningful.</span></p><p><span>If a model implements error correction (or otherwise is robust to interference noise), the sensitivity of activations to perturbations should be anisotropic: perturbations into most directions should be treated as interference noise and hence have little downstream effect, while perturbations specifically targeting a feature direction would have a much larger effect at low magnitudes. Since feature directions are a small fraction of all directions, random directions should behave like interference noise, and hence feature directions should elicit responses at smaller perturbation magnitudes than random directions.</span></p><p><span>Previous work (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LajDyGyiyX8DNNsuF/interim-research-report-activation-plateaus-and-sensitive-1"><span>Heimersheim &amp; Mendel, 2024</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sGmT6aJPY7Xzuz3Ax/characterizing-stable-regions-in-the-residual-stream-of-llms"><span>Janiak et al. 2024</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WMfSbt7AAcJdHzysB/activation-plateaus-where-and-how-they-emerge"><span>Shinkle &amp; Heimersheim 2025</span></a><span>) has observed that the downstream response to perturbations of a transformer's activations is in fact suppressed for small perturbation magnitudes ("activation plateaus").  </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dS5dSgwaDQRoWdTuu/investigating-sensitive-directions-in-gpt-2-an-improved"><span>Lee &amp; Heimersheim 2024</span></a><span> found that perturbations into SAE directions are </span><i><span>also </span></i><span>suppressed. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ioPnHKFyy4Cw2Gr2x/mechanistically-eliciting-latent-behaviors-in-language-1"><span>Mack &amp; Turner 2024</span></a><span> directly optimized directions to have a large downstream effect, and found them to act as steering vectors. Here we attempt to find other directions with a large downstream effect (features), without directly optimizing for such an effect.</span></p><h1><span>Setup</span></h1><p><span>We perturb model activations along candidate directions at an early layer and measure the downstream response at a late layer. We compare three types of directions: contrastive (difference-of-means), SAE features, and random. We now describe the experimental setup in detail.</span></p><p><b><span>Models, layers and metrics.</span></b><span> We run experiments on three models from different families: Gemma 2-9B, Llama 3.1-8B, and Qwen 3-1.7B. We perturb their activations at an early residual stream layer: layer 2 for Gemma and Llama, layer 7 for Qwen. These are the earliest layers for which we had access to SAEs for each model. It is desirable to perturb as early as possible as prior work has shown that the suppression effect is more pronounced the more layers there are between perturbation and measurement (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WMfSbt7AAcJdHzysB/activation-plateaus-where-and-how-they-emerge"><span>Shinkle &amp; Heimersheim, 2025</span></a><span>). For all models we measure the downstream response at the second-to-last residual stream layer as the L2 distance between perturbed and unperturbed activations.</span></p><p><b><span>Perturbation method.</span></b><span> All perturbations are performed on a hypersphere, i.e., keeping the activation norm constant, to isolate the effect of direction from that of norm. We perturb at constant angular speed: the x-axis of our plots shows the angle (in degrees) between the original and perturbed activation. We always perturb at the last token position.</span></p><p><b><span>Contrastive directions.</span></b><span> We construct contrastive vectors as the difference of mean activations between two classes of thirty semantically matched prompts, i.e., prompts that differ only along one concept (e.g., "The sun rises in the" in English and Mandarin). We do this for English vs. Mandarin text, Python vs. Haskell code, and male vs. female. We then perturb activations of ten held-out prompts from one class along the contrastive direction toward the other (e.g., English activations perturbed toward Mandarin).</span></p><p><b><span>SAE directions.</span></b><span> For each held-out activation (e.g., an English prompt), we identify the SAE features that activate most strongly on that input and perturb along ten of them. We also tested SAE features selected at random from the full dictionary, and features that activate strongly on the opposite class (e.g., Mandarin-activating features applied to English activations); the results were similar.</span></p><p><b><span>Random directions.</span></b><span> We construct random directions as differences between activations of randomly sampled FineWeb prompts. Random directions constructed in this way are biased towards high-variance directions in activation space, making them a stronger baseline than uniformly sampled random vectors in activation space (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LajDyGyiyX8DNNsuF/interim-research-report-activation-plateaus-and-sensitive-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>Heimersheim &amp; Mendel 2024</span></a><span>).</span></p><h1><span>Extra Results &amp; Robustness Checks</span></h1><p><span>Our main results are described in the Summary &amp; Main Results section. Here, we present variations and ablations on the experimental setup to confirm our results are robust to these choices.</span></p><p><span>First, we perform additive perturbations rather than spherical, i.e., without keeping the norm fixed and at constant linear step size rather than constant angular step size. In Figure 3 we show that our results hold across this variation, suggesting that activation norm dynamics were not driving the effect.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="plot_complete.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1774021661/lexical_client_uploads/uaxyefe16twmqrvpe6zh.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 3: Additive (non-constant-norm) perturbation on Gemma 2-9B, English→Mandarin. The x-axis shows the L</span><sup><span>2</span></sup><span> norm of the perturbation rather than the angle. </span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Second, we measure the downstream effect using metrics other than L</span><sup><span>2</span></sup><span> distance in activation space: KL-divergence in logits, and cosine distance at the same residual stream layer (defined as 1 - cosine similarity). KL-divergence measures the effect on the model's output distribution rather than on internal activations, while cosine distance isolates the directional component of the downstream change from its magnitude. Again our results remain consistent, as shown in Figure 4.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="figure4_composite.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1774021250/lexical_client_uploads/a4tzxabfgk9i2hnu293h.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 4: Alternative downstream metrics on Gemma 2-9B, English/Mandarin. Left: KL-divergence in logits. Right: cosine distance in activation space.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Finally, we perturb at a later layer, namely layer 8 rather than layer 2. Prior work has shown that the suppression effect sharpens the more layers there are between perturbation and measurement (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WMfSbt7AAcJdHzysB/activation-plateaus-where-and-how-they-emerge"><span>Shinkle &amp; Heimersheim, 2025</span></a><span>). As expected, we find that perturbing at layer 2 yields stronger plateaus than perturbing at layer 8, but the difference between contrastive and other directions remains clear (Figure 5).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="figure5_composite.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1774021287/lexical_client_uploads/lpbfrmgvtj4joqxx0no2.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 5: Effect of perturbation layer on Gemma 2-9B, English/Mandarin. Left: layer 2 (baseline, same as Figure 1). Right: layer 8.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The results remain consistent across all variations, supporting the robustness of our findings.</span></p><h1><span>Code and Data</span></h1><p><span>All code and data supporting this post are available at </span><a href="https://github.com/FranciscoHS/contrastive-vs-sae-perturbation"><span>github.com/FranciscoHS/contrastive-vs-sae-perturbation</span></a><span>.</span></p><h1><span>Future Work</span></h1><p><span>We're excited about following up on this work along three main directions:</span></p><ol><li><span>We might have a test for whether a direction is a feature. What can we do with it? An obvious example is using our test as an unsupervised optimization criterion for finding feature-like directions (e.g. additional SAE loss terms; </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ioPnHKFyy4Cw2Gr2x/mechanistically-eliciting-latent-behaviors-in-language-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>MELBO</span></a><span> extensions). We’re keen on other ways to make use of this test!</span></li><li><span>We've looked at our results from an error correction perspective. Can we distinguish the error correction explanation (in the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05451"><span>Computation in Superposition</span></a><span> sense) from other robustness explanations (e.g. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/lawrencec?mention=user"><span>@LawrenceC</span></a><span>'s comment </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LajDyGyiyX8DNNsuF/interim-research-report-activation-plateaus-and-sensitive-1?commentId=ngdwsJCpTncG2NLDz"><span>here</span></a><span>)? Can we test theories of computation in neural networks? Does error correction predict scaling laws, e.g. for the model response as a function of the number of perturbed features?</span></li><li><span>Can we test assumptions about the geometry of activation space, in the context of activation plateaus? Are feature representations discrete (e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25596"><span>binary</span></a><span>) or continuous? Does the set of activations lie on a vector space or a (non-Euclidean) manifold?</span></li></ol><h1><span>Acknowledgements</span></h1><p><span>This work was funded by the </span><a href="https://www.pivotal-research.org/fellowship" rel="noreferrer"><span>Pivotal Fellowship</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Thanks to Ariel Gil, Reilly Haskins, Arnau Marin-Llobet, Motahareh Sohrabi, Zheng (Zack) Hui and Andrew Mack for helpful discussions.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrntk87ryioc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrntk87ryioc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span> We construct random directions by randomly sampling FineWeb prompts and computing the difference between their activations.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne8aykfazgm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe8aykfazgm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span> Layer 2 for the Gemma and Llama models, 7 for Qwen. Layer 7 is the earliest layer for which we had access to an SAE for the Qwen model.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QrPxNDprJwXiE73Py/finding-features-in-transformers-contrastive-directions#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Untrusted Monitoring is Default; Trusted Monitoring is not](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b5oHr5TrfQzCBZdXW/untrusted-monitoring-is-default-trusted-monitoring-is-not)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><b><span>These views are my own and not necessarily representative of those of any colleagues with whom I have worked on AI control.</span></b></i></p><p><i><span>TL;DR: It's much cheaper and quicker to just throw some honeypots at your monitor models than to robustly prove trustedness for every model you want to use. Therefore I think the most likely future involves untrusted monitoring with some monitor validation as a default path.</span></i></p><p><span>This post talks about two different ways of monitoring AI systems, trusted monitoring, and untrusted monitoring. You can find papers distinguishing between them </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2312.06942" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.10374" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.20628" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>, a video </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0pgEMWy70Qk" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>, or you can look at this graphic which didn't make it into Gardner-Challis et. al. (2026):</span></p><img alt="Abstract.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1774000302/lexical_client_uploads/vemn0vs51gl80u8qqqdy.png" /><h2><span>Context</span></h2><p><span>From when I started working at untrusted monitoring until very recently</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8gw6vkx8dvp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8gw6vkx8dvp">[1]</a></sup></span><span>, I assumed that trusted monitoring was the default policy that AI companies would use, and that untrusted monitoring was an exotic, high-effort policy which would be much less likely to be used.</span></p><p><span>Now I think it's the other way round. </span></p><h2><span>Full Trustedness is Hard</span></h2><p><span>The distinction between an untrusted model and a trusted one exists in the map. A trusted model is normally described as one for which we have</span><b><span> strong</span></b><span> evidence that it isn't coherently pursuing long-term misaligned goals. This kind of evidence is very difficult to find; mostly it just boils down to arguments from the model's inability to scheme very well.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LhxHcASQwpNa3mRNk/untrusted-smart-models-and-trusted-dumb-models" rel="noreferrer"><span>Buck has written about</span></a><span> how this means we'll hit a ceiling on our ability to trust models: below that, the inability arguments hold and the models can be trusted; above it, we don't know.</span></p><p><span>In that scenario, somebody (AI companies?) is trying their best to assess and report trustedness. They are empowered to use a large amount of resources to get a good picture of the problem, and are empowered to throw their hands up and say "Yep, this model is beyond our evaluations, we cannot trust it." Their reports give a picture with a fairly clean divide, like the Agent-3/Agent-4 divide in </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>AI 2027</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>There are a few reasons I don't expect this to happen.</span></p><ul><li><span>It's a lot of effort to run a high-quality eval regularly. OpenAI, Anthropic, and GDM have started to release models in units of 0.1, and have scaled back their capabilities evals to match. Claude has maxed out several rule-out evals, so its AI R&amp;D capabilities were assessed by interviewing some Anthropic employees. I expect trustedness evals to suffer the same fate, giving blurry results.</span></li><li><span>It's bad PR, both internally and externally, for a company to come out and say "We definitely cannot trust this model." which means that information about untrustedness will probably be obfuscated in external </span><i><span>and internal</span></i><span> discussions.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item"><ul><li><span>Less cynically, people might just have honest disagreements about trustedness within the AI company.</span></li></ul></li><li><span>Internal disagreements on whether the model is untrusted will prevent it from getting a clear stamp of "First untrusted model."</span></li></ul><p><span>What this means is that we'll see a slow slide, which has probably already begun (see the </span><a href="https://x.com/apolloaievals/status/1972744729935876286" rel="noreferrer"><span>Apollo Claude Sonnet 4.5 Eval</span></a><span>) from a regime in which models are generally trusted, to a regime in which they generally aren't. I do not expect any company (especially not ones which are not Anthropic) to ever draw a clear line between trusted and untrusted models.</span></p><p><span>Instead, I think the most likely scenario is that the employees working day-to-day do not really trust their most recent model, and at the same time they don't share a clear picture of which past models they </span><b><span>do</span></b><span> trust. Therefore, I expect the models being used for monitoring won't be trusted.</span></p><h2><span>Untrusted Monitoring is Just Limited-Scope Trusted Monitoring</span></h2><p><span>This is another way I've come to think about untrusted monitoring.</span></p><p><span>When we "trust" an AI in a specific context, we in practice mean something like "We've evaled it hard enough that we think we can predict the important parts about its behaviour when it's deployed </span><i><span>in some setting</span></i><span>." and if that context is "everything" we call it a "trusted model".</span></p><p><span>The fundamental barrier to trusting a model is the distributional shift between training/evaluation and deployment, which covers things like eval-awareness, adversaries looking to jailbreak it, and other stuff.</span></p><p><span>Most untrusted monitoring protocols include a step where we validate the monitor against a bunch of honeypots. This is just a fancy training/eval phase. Instead of building broad-scoped high effort alignment datasets and evals, you only do so for the narrow task of monitoring. You also deliberately remove information (signal jamming) to make training/evaluation and deployment more similar, to reduce distributional shift.</span></p><p><span>After successful validation, an untrusted monitor is just a model which is trusted in the limited task of monitoring. Validating an untrusted monitor is less effort than trying to convincingly prove that a given model is trusted, and human effort is probably the bottleneck.</span></p><h2><span>Cost and Speed</span></h2><p><span>One point often raised in favour of trusting monitoring is cost. The weaker trusted model, so the argument goes, will be cheaper to run than the stronger untrusted model, and probably faster too.</span></p><p><span>This might just be false! Claude Opus 4.5 is cheaper and faster than Opus 4. The GPT-5.n series has gotten much stronger without getting any more expensive or slower. In fact, for thinking models, it's sometimes </span><i><span>cheaper and faster</span></i><span> to run the N.m+1 than to run the N.m, since it ends up using fewer thinking tokens.</span></p><p><span>Either way, the cost of internal deployment is just not that high compared to the salaries (and onboarding costs, etc.) of the employees using the model, the model training costs, the experimental budgets of the labs, at least for now.</span></p><h1><span>Monitoring is Here!</span></h1><p><span>We've actually seen both trusted-like and untrusted-like monitoring already.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/constitutional-classifiers" rel="noreferrer"><span>On the trusted-like side, Anthropic uses constitutional classifiers based on Haiku-sized models to defend against jailbreaks.</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/syB2r2X4E7nmw58co/openai-how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-for" rel="noreferrer"><span>On the untrusted-like side, OpenAI (as far as I can tell from the post) is using their best models to monitor their best models. </span></a></p><p><span>I think the OpenAI case is typical of what we'll see in the future for serious monitoring. Pretty much the best model they have (GPT-5.4) will be used to monitor the actions of other instances of their coding agents. If they get suspicious of their model scheming, they'll design some honeypots and do some monitor validation, rather than try and grapple with what trustedness actually means for their models. Monitoring honeypots might well be a part of the validation process that OpenAI is already using for their GPT-5.4 model.</span></p><p><span>Setting up and executing honeypots is mostly an engineering and empirical problem, which can be done by an internal team. Declaring individual models "trusted" and "untrusted" has a lot more freight and complications. I expect the former to remain the path of least resistance for AI control into the future. </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8gw6vkx8dvp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8gw6vkx8dvp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Ten minutes ago, when I started writing this post.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b5oHr5TrfQzCBZdXW/untrusted-monitoring-is-default-trusted-monitoring-is-not#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Does Hebrew Have Verbs?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/e8XnJFw9noBXoXFWr/does-hebrew-have-verbs)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Spinoza's <em><a href="https://archive.org/details/ned-kbn-all-00004303-001">Compendium of Hebrew Grammar</a></em> (1677, posthumous, unfinished) claims that all Hebrew words, except a few particles, are nouns. The standard scholarly reaction is that this is either a metaphysical imposition (projecting his monistic ontology onto grammar) or a terminological trick (defining "noun" so broadly it's vacuous). Both wrongly project Greek and Latin grammatical categories as the neutral baseline.</p>
<h1>Spinoza's Claim: Hebrew's All Nouns</h1>
<p>From Chapter 5 of the <em>Compendium</em> (<a href="https://books.google.com/books/about/Hebrew_grammar.html?id=ai1iAAAAMAAJ">Bloom translation, 1962</a>):</p>
<blockquote>
<p>"By a noun I understand a word by which we signify or indicate something that is understood. However, among things that are understood there can be either things and attributes of things, modes and relationships, or actions, and modes and relationships of actions."</p>
</blockquote>
<p>And:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>"For all Hebrew words, except for a few interjections and conjunctions and one or two particles, have the force and properties of nouns. Because the grammarians did not understand this they considered many words to be irregular which according to the usage of language are most regular."</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The word "noun" here is <em>nomen</em>. It means "name." Spinoza is saying: almost every Hebrew word is a name for something understood. This includes names for actions, names for relationships, names for attributes. His taxonomy of intelligible content explicitly includes actions and modes of actions alongside things and attributes.</p>
<h1>The Vacuousness Objection</h1>
<p>The obvious objection is: if "noun" covers actions as well as things, then the claim that "all words are nouns" is trivially true and does no work. Any content word names something intelligible; so what?</p>
<p>But this objection assumes that a useful grammar <em>must</em> draw a hard categorical line between nouns and verbs, and that Spinoza's refusal to draw it is therefore vacuous. That assumption is embedded in the Greek grammatical tradition; it is not a fact about Hebrew.</p>
<h1>Semitic Roots</h1>
<p>In Hebrew (and Arabic, Akkadian, and other <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_root">Semitic languages</a>), words are generated from consonantal roots—typically trilateral—by applying vowel patterns and affixes. The root כ-ת-ב generates <em>katav</em> (he wrote), <em>kotev</em> (one who writes), <em>ktav</em> (writing/script), <em>mikhtav</em> (letter), <em>katvan</em> (scribbler). The morphological operation is the same in every case: take the root, apply a pattern that describes the relation of the concept to the thing you are describing. For example, <em>mikhtav</em> is something that is made-written, a letter, much like the Arabic <em>mameluke</em> is someone who is made-owned, a slave. Whether the output functions as what a Greek grammarian would call a "noun" or a "verb" depends on which pattern you applied, not on some fundamentally different generative process.</p>
<p>This is not how Greek or Latin works. In those languages, nouns and verbs belong to largely separate inflectional systems (though they do have participles). Nouns decline for case and number; verbs conjugate for tense, aspect, mood, and person. A Greek speaker can usually tell from a word's form alone which category it belongs to. The noun/verb distinction corresponds to a real difference in morphological machinery.</p>
<p>In Hebrew, it doesn't. The grammarians who insisted on the distinction—both the rabbinical grammarians working in the Arabic tradition and the Christian Hebraists working from Latin—were forcing Hebrew into a framework designed for languages with a different structure. The result, as Spinoza observed, was that regular Hebrew forms got classified as irregular, because they didn't respect a boundary the language doesn't draw.</p>
<h2>The infinitive</h2>
<p>Spinoza argues (Chapter 5) that the Hebrew infinitive "is a pure unadulterated noun" that "knows nothing about the present, nor past, nor any time whatever." In Latin, the infinitive carries tense. Amare is present ("to love"), amavisse is perfect ("to have loved"). So grammarians working from Latin categories classified the Hebrew infinitive as a verb form, and then had to treat it as deficient because it lacks the tense marking a "verb" should have. But Spinoza says it doesn't carry tense because it isn't a verb. It's a name for an action. (The Hebrew metalinguistic term for "infinitive" is shem hapo'al, literally "name of the action.") The "irregularity" disappears when you stop expecting it to behave like a Latin infinitive.</p>
<h2>The passive reflexive.</h2>
<p>In Chapter XXI, Spinoza identifies a verb form he says "seems to be unknown to all the grammarians whom I know." The specific example is Ezekiel 23:48, where the form וְנִוַּסְּרוּ (weniwwasseru, "that they may be taught") appears. This is what modern grammarians now call the Nithpael, a passive reflexive combining features of the Niphal and Hithpael.
Here's how one gets there. We start with a root י-ס-ר (yasar) indicating the idea of applying discipline. Render it reflexive and it's hithyasser, meaning "applies self-discipline". Then apply the passive and it means "made to be self-disciplining."</p>
<p>From a Greco-Latin perspective you encounter this word and the first question you ask is, "which schema do I apply? Is it a verb? Is it a noun?" and it's hard to answer. But it's a whole compound predicate. It's unusual to do that by just stacking two operators on a root even in Hebrew, but it's not as weird as it would be if you had to first categorize a word as a verb or a noun, and then apply the appropriate modification within that data structure.</p>
<p>The standard framework of Spinoza's era recognized seven binyanim (standard modifications of a root) and this form didn't fit any of them, so grammarians either emended it as a scribal error or classified it as anomalous. Spinoza argues it's a regular pattern the grammarians couldn't see because their framework didn't have a slot for it. Modern Hebrew grammar now recognizes the Nithpael as a rare but real stem formation.</p>
<h1>Where the noun/verb distinction comes from</h1>
<p>The Arabic grammatical tradition, which the medieval rabbinical Hebrew grammarians adopted wholesale, classifies words into three categories: <em>ism</em> (noun/name), <em>fi'l</em> (verb/action), and <em>ḥarf</em> (particle). Scholars have long noted the parallel between this trichotomy and Aristotle's division of speech into <em>onoma</em> (name), <em>rhema</em> (verb/predicate), and <em>sundesmos</em> (connective); Syriac scholars were important intermediaries in transmitting Greek linguistic thought to Arabic, though the degree of direct dependence remains debated.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> The classification reached Hebrew grammar through two independent routes: Greek → Latin → Christian Hebraists, and Greek → Arabic → rabbinical grammarians. Both paths originate in Greek philosophy.</p>
<p>Around 100 BC, in the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Art_of_Grammar"><em>Art of Grammar</em></a> attributed to Dionysius Thrax, <em>rhēma</em> was redefined to mean something more like the modern colloquial sense of "verb": "a part of speech without case inflection, but inflected for tense, person, and number, signifying an activity or process performed or undergone."<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span> This is the framework the Arabic and Roman grammarians inherited.</p>
<p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_ben_David_Hayyuj">Judah ben David Hayyuj</a> (c. 945–1000), the founder of scientific Hebrew grammar, applied Arabic grammatical theory to Hebrew, including the <em>ism</em>/<em>fi'l</em>/<em>ḥarf</em> trichotomy and the principle that all Hebrew roots are trilateral.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> His technical terms were translations of Arabic grammatical terms. <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonah_ibn_Janah">Jonah ibn Janah</a> (c. 990–1055) extended this work, producing the first complete Hebrew grammar and drawing explicitly from the Arabic grammatical works of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sibawayhi">Sibawayh</a> and al-Mubarrad.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span> When Spinoza complained that "the grammarians" misunderstood Hebrew, this is the tradition he was arguing against.</p>
<p>Aristotle's noun/verb distinction is not just a grammatical observation. It reflects his substance/predication ontology. The world consists of substances (things that exist independently) and predicates (things said <em>about</em> substances). A noun names a substance; a verb predicates something of it. The sentence "Socrates runs" has the structure: substance + predication. The grammar encodes the metaphysics.</p>
<h1>Dramas and Graphs</h1>
<p>Greek and similar languages have different pools of words for filling the grammatical roles of noun and verb. Hebrew has one pool of roots that supplies words for both roles, depending on the pattern applied. These aren't just two different ways of doing the same thing. They reflect different structural priorities.</p>
<p>The Indo-European system is built around assembling a scene: placing distinct actors into relationships with distinct actions. You need different building blocks for the actors and the actions because they play different structural roles in the scene. Who did what to whom, when, in what manner. Case endings on nouns tell you the role; verb conjugation tells you the temporal and modal frame. The grammar presupposes that the actor/action distinction is primitive.</p>
<p>The Semitic system works differently. Each root is a node in a flat graph of intelligibles. The graph doesn't recurse; roots refer to intelligible things, not to relations between other roots. And it doesn't privilege any type of node over any other, which is why the morphological system treats them all with the same machinery. It does not start by assigning one word the role of "the thing" and another the role of "what the thing does."</p>
<p>A sentence picks out some nodes from this graph, and casts them into some definite relation to each other. Their arrangement and patterns of modification describe the way in which these intelligibles are related: process, agent, result, instrument, quality, location.</p>
<p>When you take the Greek-derived framework and impose it on Hebrew, you're asking a flat graph of intelligibles to behave like a scene-assembly system. The spurious irregularities Spinoza complained about are projections of the friction from this mismatch.</p>
<h1>I'm not projecting, you're projecting!</h1>
<p>The standard scholarly line is that Spinoza projected his philosophical commitments onto his grammar; that his monism (one substance, everything else is modes) motivated his claim that Hebrew has one part of speech with subcategories rather than two fundamentally different parts of speech. Harvey (2002) argues that the <em>Compendium</em>'s linguistic categories parallel the conceptual categories of the <em>Ethics</em>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span> Rozenberg (2025) goes further, claiming Spinoza "project[ed] the characteristics of Latin onto Hebrew" and thereby "neglected the dynamism of Hebrew."<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span> Stracenski provides a more sympathetic reading but still frames the question as whether the <em>Compendium</em> serves the <em>Ethics</em>' metaphysics or the <em>Tractatus</em>' hermeneutics.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-7">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>This gets the direction of explanation backwards, or at least sideways. Spinoza was reading a Semitic language and describing how it generates words. The fact that his description aligns with his metaphysics may reflect a common cause: both the grammar and the metaphysics are what you get when you don't take the Aristotelian actor/action distinction as a primitive. Spinoza rejected Aristotle's substance/predicate ontology in the <em>Ethics</em>; he also noticed that Aristotle's noun/verb grammar didn't fit Hebrew.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-1"><p>Aristotle divides <em>lexis</em> into <em>onoma</em>, <em>rhema</em>, and <em>sundesmos</em> in <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetics_(Aristotle)"><em>Poetics</em> 1456b–1457a</a>. Farina documents how this tripartite scheme reached Arabic grammar via Syriac translations of Aristotle's <em>Organon</em>, with Syriac Christians serving as intermediaries between Greek and Arabic linguistic thought. See Margherita Farina, <a href="https://hal.science/hal-04782608v1/document">"The interactions between the Syriac, Arabic and Greek traditions,"</a> <em>International Encyclopedia of Language and Linguistics</em>, Third Edition, 2025. The question of whether Sibawayh's <em>ism</em>/<em>fi'l</em>/<em>ḥarf</em> directly derives from Aristotle or represents independent development remains <a href="https://brill.com/view/journals/ow/1/1/article-p1_4.xml?language=en">actively debated</a>; the structural parallels are clear even if the exact transmission pathway is contested. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-2"><p>The <em>Art of Grammar</em> was translated into Syriac by Joseph Huzaya (c. 6th century) and became a key conduit for Greek grammatical categories reaching the Arabic tradition. See the <a href="https://gedsh.bethmardutho.org/Dionysius-Thrax">Gorgias Encyclopedic Dictionary entry on Dionysius Thrax</a>. On the disputed authorship of the <em>Technē</em>, see the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysius_Thrax">Wikipedia article</a>: modern scholars generally accept that the eight-part-of-speech classification was compiled under Thrax's name but may postdate him by several decades. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-3"><p>On Hayyuj's application of Arabic grammar to Hebrew and his establishment of the trilateral root principle, see the <a href="https://www.jewishencyclopedia.com/articles/12823-root">Jewish Encyclopedia entry on "Root"</a>. His <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_ben_David_Hayyuj">Wikipedia biography</a> notes that "the technical terms still employed in current Hebrew grammars are most of them simply translations of the Arabic terms employed by Hayyuj." <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-4"><p>Ibn Janah's <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonah_ibn_Janah"><em>Kitab al-Luma</em></a> was the first complete Hebrew grammar. It drew from Arabic grammatical works including those of Sibawayh and al-Mubarrad. See also the <a href="https://www.jewishvirtuallibrary.org/hebrew-linguistic-literature">Jewish Virtual Library entry on Hebrew linguistic literature</a>. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-5"><p>Warren Zev Harvey, "Spinoza's Metaphysical Hebraism," in Heidi M. Ravven and Lenn E. Goodman, eds., <a href="https://sunypress.edu/Books/J/Jewish-Themes-in-Spinoza-s-Philosophy"><em>Jewish Themes in Spinoza's Philosophy</em></a> (Albany: SUNY Press, 2002), 107–114. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-6"><p>Jacques J. Rozenberg, <a href="https://philarchive.org/rec/ROZSCI">"Spinoza's Compendium: Between Hebrew and Latin Grammars of the Middle Ages and the Renaissance, Verbs versus Nouns,"</a> <em>International Philosophical Quarterly</em>, online first, October 26, 2025, DOI: 10.5840/ipq20251024258. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-7"><p>Inja Stracenski, <a href="http://www.parrhesiajournal.org/parrhesia32/parrhesia32_stracenski.pdf">"Spinoza's Compendium of the Grammar of the Hebrew Language,"</a> <em>Parrhesia</em> 32. Stracenski notes the divide between historical approaches (Klijnsmit, placing Spinoza within Jewish grammatical tradition) and philosophical approaches (Harvey, connecting the <em>Compendium</em> to the <em>Ethics</em>). See also Guadalupe González Diéguez's <a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1002/9781119538349.ch45">companion chapter</a> in <em>A Companion to Spinoza</em> (Wiley, 2021) and Steven Nadler, "Aliquid remanet: What Are We to Do with Spinoza's Compendium of Hebrew Grammar?" <em>Journal of the History of Philosophy</em> 56, no. 1 (2018): 155–167. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hhiKqNtG6dAMCNpmA-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/e8XnJFw9noBXoXFWr/does-hebrew-have-verbs#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: March 19](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-03-19.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>David Poll points out the <a href="https://www.davidpoll.com/2026/02/code-review-is-not-about-catching-bugs/">flawed premise of the argument that code review is a bottleneck</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>To be fair, finding defects has always been listed as a goal of code review – Wikipedia will tell you as much. And sure, reviewers do catch bugs. But I think that framing dramatically overstates the bug-catching role and understates everything else code review does. If your review process is primarily a bug-finding mechanism, you’re leaving most of the value on the table.</p>

  <p>Code review answers: “Should this be part of my product?”</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That’s close to how I think about it. I think of code review as primarily about keeping the code base healthy. And although many people think of code review as <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/branching-patterns.html#reviewed-commits">pre-integration review</a> done on pull requests, I look at code review as a broader activity both done earlier <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/PairProgramming.html">(Pair Programming)</a> and later <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/RefinementCodeReview.html">(Refinement Code Review)</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>At Firebase, I spent 5.5 years running an API council…</p>

  <p>The most valuable feedback from that council was never “you have a bug in this spec.” It was “this API implies a mental model that contradicts what you shipped last quarter” or “this deprecation strategy will cost more trust than the improvement is worth” or simply “a developer encountering this for the first time won’t understand what it does.” Those are judgment calls about whether something should be part of the product – the same fundamental question that code review answers at a different altitude. No amount of production observability surfaces them, because the system can work perfectly and still be the wrong thing to have built.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>His overall point is that code review is all about applying judgment, steering the code in a good direction. AI raises the level of that judgment, focusing review on more important things.</p>

<p>I agree that we shouldn’t be thinking of review as a bug-catching mechanism, and that it’s about steering the code base. In addition I’d also add that it’s about communication between people, enabling multiple perspectives on the development of the product. This is true both for code review, and for pair programming.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://www.honeycomb.io/blog/production-is-where-the-rigor-goes">Charity Majors is unhappy</a> with me and rest of the folks that attended the Thoughtworks Future of Software Development Retreat.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>But the longer I sit with this recap, the more troubled I am by what it doesn’t say. I worry that the most respected minds in software are unintentionally replicating a serious blind spot that has haunted software engineering for decades: relegating production to the realm of bugs and incidents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There are lots of things we didn’t discuss in that day-and-a-half, and it’s understandable that a topic that matters so deeply to her is visible by its absence. I’m certainly not speaking for anyone else who was there, but I’ll take the opportunity to share some of my thoughts on this.</p>

<p>I consider observability to be a key tool in working with our AI future. As she points out, observability isn’t really about finding bugs - although I’ve long been a supporter of the notion of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/qa-in-production.html">QA in Production</a>. Observability is about revealing what the system actually does, when in the hands of its actual users. Test cases help you deal with the known paths, but reality has a habit of taking you into the unknowns, not just the unknowns of the software’s behavior in unforeseen places, but also the unknowns of how the software affects the broader human and organizational systems it’s embedded into. By watching how software is used, we can learn about what users really want to achieve, these <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/ObservedRequirement.html">observed requirements</a> are often things that never popped up in interviews and focus groups.</p>

<p>If these unknown territories are true in systems written line-by-line in deterministic code, it’s even more true when code is written in a world of <a href="https://annievella.com/posts/the-middle-loop/">supervisory engineering</a> where humans are no longer to look over every semi-colon. Certainly <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/harness-engineering.html">harness engineering</a> and <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/humans-and-agents.html">humans in the loop</a> help, and I’m as much a fan as ever about the importance of 
tests as a way to both explain and evaluate the code. But these unknowns will inevitably raise the importance of observability and its role to understand what the system thinks it does. I think it’s likely we’ll see a future where much of a developer’s effort is figuring what a system is doing and why it’s behaving that way, where observability tools are the IDE.</p>

<p>In this I ponder the lesson of AI playing Go. AlphaGo defeated the best humans a decade ago, and since then <a href="https://www.technologyreview.com/2026/02/27/1133624/ai-is-rewiring-how-the-worlds-best-go-players-think/">humans study AI</a> to become better players and maybe discover some broader principles. I’m intrigued by how humans can learn from AI systems to be improve in other fields, where success is less deterministically defined.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Tim Requarth questions the portrayal of <a href="https://timrequarth.substack.com/p/silicon-valleys-mythology-of-human">AI as an amplifier</a> for human cognition. He considers the different way we navigate with GPS compared to maps.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If you unfold a paper map, you study the streets, trace a route, convert the bird’s-eye abstraction into the first-person POV of actually walking—and by the time you arrived, you’d have a nascent mental model of how the city fits together. Or you could fire up Google Maps: A blue dot, an optimal line from A to B, a reassuring robotic voice telling you when to turn. You follow, you arrive, you have no idea, really, where you are. A paper map demands something from you, and that demand leaves you with knowledge. GPS requires nothing, and leaves you with nothing. A paper map and GPS are tools with the same purpose, but opposite cognitive consequences.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He introduces some attractive metaphors here. Steve Jobs called computers “bicycles for the mind”, Satya Nadella said with the launch of ChatGPT that “we went from the bicycle to the steam engine”.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Like another 19th-century invention, the steam locomotive, the bicycle was a technological revolution. But a train traveler sat back and enjoyed the ride, while a cyclist still had to put in effort. With a bicycle, “you are traveling,” wrote a cycling enthusiast in 1878, “not being traveled.”</p>
</blockquote>

<p>In both examples, there’s a difference between tools that extend capability and tools that replace it. The question is what we lose when we are passive in the journey? He argues that Silicon Valley executives are too focused on the goal, and ignoring the cognitive atrophy that happens to the humans being traveled.</p>

<p>Much of this depends, I think, on whether we care about what we are losing. I struggle with mental arithmetic, so I value calculators, whether on my phone or <code>M-x calc</code>. I don’t think I lose anything when I let the machine handle the toil of calculation. I share missing the sense of place when using a GPS over a map, but am happy that I can now drive though Lynn without getting lost.
And when it comes to writing, I have no desire to let an LLM write this page.</p>

      Anchoring</a> externalizes the decision context into a living
      document.</p>
